**Greeks** or **Hellenes** ([/ˈhɛliːnz/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/English); [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language): Έλληνες, *Éllines* [\[ˈelines\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Greek)) are an [ethnic group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_group) and [nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nation) native to [Greece](12108.md), [Cyprus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Cypriots), [southern Albania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greeks_in_Albania), [Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greeks_in_Turkey#History), [southern Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greeks_in_Italy), parts of [Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Greeks), and to a lesser extent, other countries surrounding the [Eastern Mediterranean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Mediterranean) and the [Black Sea](3386.md). They also form a significant [diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_diaspora) (*omogenia*) around the world.[^cite_note-Roberts1-45]

Greek communities have been historically established on the shores of the [Mediterranean Sea](19006.md) and the Black Sea, but the Greek people themselves have always been concentrated on the [Aegean Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aegean_Sea) and the [Ionian Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ionian_Sea), where the [Greek language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language) has been spoken since the [Bronze Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronze_Age).[^cite_note-46][^cite_note-Sutton-47] Until the early 20th century, Greeks were distributed between the [Greek peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_peninsula), the western coast of [Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolia) as well as [Cappadocia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cappadocia), the Black Sea coast, Egypt, the [Balkans](4829.md), Cyprus, and [Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantinople).[^cite_note-Sutton-47] Many of these regions coincided to a large extent with the borders of the [Byzantine Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire) of the late 11th century and the Eastern Mediterranean areas of [ancient Greek colonization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek_colonization).[^cite_note-48] The cultural centres of the Greeks have included [Athens](1216.md), [Thessalonica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessalonica), [Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandria), [Smyrna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smyrna), and Constantinople at various periods.

In more recent history, most ethnic Greeks have lived within the borders of the modern Greek state or in Cyprus, as the [Greek genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_genocide) and subsequent [Greek–Turkish population exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek%E2%80%93Turkish_population_exchange) nearly ended the three-millennia-old Greek presence across Asia Minor. Other longstanding Greek populations can be found from southern Italy to the [Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasus_Greeks), southern [Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greeks_in_Russia) and [Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greeks_in_Ukraine), and in the Greek diaspora communities in a number of other countries, especially in the [Western world](21208200.md). Today, most religious Greeks are [Christians](18337522.md) officially registered with the [Greek Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Church).[^cite_note-CIA-49]

The Greek people have greatly influenced and contributed to [culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_culture), [visual arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Greek_art), exploration, [theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_theatre), [literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_literature), [philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_philosophy), ethics, politics, [architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_architecture), [music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_music), [mathematics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_mathematics),[^cite_note-50] [medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_medicine), [science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_science), [technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_technology), commerce, [cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_cuisine), and [sports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_Greece). The Greek language, which constitutes [an independent branch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenic_languages) within the [Indo-European language family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-European_language_family), is the [oldest recorded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_languages_by_first_written_account) living language,[^cite_note-51] and its vocabulary has been the basis of many languages ([including English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_words_of_Greek_origin)) and the [international scientific nomenclature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_scientific_nomenclature). As early as the 4th century BC, the Greek language spread to become the most widely spoken *[lingua franca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lingua_franca)* in the [Mediterranean world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean_world) for over a millennium, earning [effective co-official status](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_East_and_Latin_West) in the [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire) and also being the language in which the [New Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Testament) of the [Christian Bible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bible) was originally written.[^cite_note-52]

## History

Further information: [History of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Greece)



Proto-Greek area of settlement (2200/2100–1900 BC) suggested by Katona (2000), Sakelariou (2016, 1980, 1975) and Phylaktopoulos (1975)



[Mycenaean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mycenaean_Greece) [funeral mask](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_mask) known as "[Mask of Agamemnon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mask_of_Agamemnon)", 16th century BC

The Greeks speak the [Greek language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language), which forms its own unique branch within the [Indo-European](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-European_languages) family of languages, the [Hellenic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenic_languages).[^cite_note-Sutton-47] They are part of a group of classical ethnicities. [Anthony D. Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthony_D._Smith) described them as an "archetypal diaspora people".[^cite_note-53][^cite_note-54]

### Origins

Further information: [Proto-Greek language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-Greek_language), [List of Ancient Greek tribes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Ancient_Greek_tribes), and [Ancient Greek religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek_religion)

The Proto-Greeks probably arrived in the area now called Greece, in the southern tip of the [Balkan Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balkan_Peninsula), sometime during the [European Bronze Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronze_Age_Europe) (c. [3rd millennium BC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/3rd_millennium_BC)).[^cite_note-55] The sequence of migrations into the Greek mainland has to be reconstructed on the basis of the [ancient Greek dialects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek_dialects), as they presented themselves centuries later and are therefore subject to some uncertainties. There were at least two migrations, the first being the [Ionians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ionians) and [Achaeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achaeans_\(tribe\)), which resulted in [Mycenaean Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mycenaean_Greece) by the 16th century BC,[^cite_note-Brit1-56][^cite_note-57] and the second, the [Dorian invasion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dorian_invasion), around the 11th century BC, displacing the [Arcadocypriot dialects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arcadocypriot_Greek), which descended from the Mycenaean period.[^cite_note-58]

### Mycenaean Greece

Main article: [Mycenaean Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mycenaean_Greece)

In c. 1600 BC, the Mycenaean Greeks borrowed from the [Minoan civilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minoan_civilization) its syllabic writing system ([Linear A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linear_A)) and developed their own [syllabic script](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syllabic_script) known as [Linear B](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linear_B),[^cite_note-Britannica-59] providing the first and oldest written evidence of [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language).[^cite_note-Britannica-59][^cite_note-60] The Mycenaeans quickly penetrated the [Aegean Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aegean_Sea) and, by the 15th century BC, had reached [Rhodes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhodes), [Crete](6591.md), [Cyprus](5593.md) and the shores of [Asia Minor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolia).[^cite_note-Sutton-47][^cite_note-61]

Around 1200 BC, the [Dorians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dorians), another Greek-speaking people, followed from [Epirus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epirus).[^cite_note-62] Older historical research often proposed [Dorian invasion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dorian_invasion) caused the collapse of the [Mycenaean civilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mycenaean_civilization), but this narrative has been abandoned in all contemporary research. It is likely that one of the factors which contributed to the Mycenaean palatial collapse was linked to raids by groups known in historiography as the "[Sea Peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sea_Peoples)" who sailed into the eastern Mediterranean around 1180 BC.[^cite_note-63] The Dorian invasion was followed by a poorly attested period of migrations, appropriately called the [Greek Dark Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Dark_Ages), but by 800 BC the landscape of [Archaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archaic_Greece) and [Classical Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Greece) was discernible.[^cite_note-Castleden2-64]

The Greeks of classical antiquity idealized their Mycenaean ancestors and the Mycenaean period as a glorious era of heroes, closeness of the gods and material wealth.[^cite_note-65] The [Homeric Epics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homer) (i.e. *[Iliad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iliad)* and *[Odyssey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Odyssey)*) were especially and generally accepted as part of the Greek past and it was not until the time of [Euhemerism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euhemerism) that scholars began to question Homer's historicity.[^cite_note-Castleden2-64] As part of the Mycenaean heritage that survived, the names of the gods and goddesses of Mycenaean Greece (e.g. [Zeus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zeus), [Poseidon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poseidon) and [Hades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hades)) became major figures of the [Olympian Pantheon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelve_Olympians) of later antiquity.[^cite_note-66]

### Ancient Greeks

Main articles: [Archaic Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archaic_Greece), [Classical Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Greece), [Ancient Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greece), and [Greek colonization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_colonization)







The three great philosophers of the classical era: [Socrates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socrates), [Plato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plato) and [Aristotle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotle)

The [ethnogenesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnogenesis) of a common Hellenic identity is linked to the development of Pan-Hellenism in the 8th century BC.[^cite_note-67] According to some scholars, the foundational event was the [Olympic Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Olympic_Games) in 776 BC, when the idea of a common Hellenism among the Greek tribes was first translated into a shared cultural experience and Hellenism was primarily a matter of common culture.[^cite_note-Roberts1-45] The works of [Homer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homer) (i.e. *[Iliad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iliad)* and *[Odyssey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Odyssey)*) and [Hesiod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hesiod) (i.e. *[Theogony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theogony)*) were written in the 8th century BC, becoming the basis of the [ancient Greek religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek_religion), ethos, history and mythology.[^cite_note-68] The [Oracle of Apollo at Delphi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pythia) was established in this period.[^cite_note-69]

The [classical period](252905.md) of Greek civilization covers a time spanning from the early 5th century BC to the [death of Alexander the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_of_Alexander_the_Great), in 323 BC (some authors prefer to split this period into "Classical", from the end of the [Greco-Persian Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greco-Persian_Wars) to the end of the Peloponnesian War, and "Fourth Century", up to the death of Alexander). It is so named because it set the standards by which Greek civilization would be judged in later eras.[^cite_note-70] The Classical period is also described as the "Golden Age" of Greek civilization, and its art, philosophy, architecture and literature would be instrumental in the formation and development of Western culture.

At the battles of [Marathon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Marathon), [Thermopylae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Thermopylae), [Salamis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Salamis), and [Platea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Platea), some Greek city-states formed a victorious alliance led by [Sparta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sparta) and [Athens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Athens). The [Delian League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delian_League), under the leadership of Athens, continued the war with the [Achaemenid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achaemenid_Empire) after the end of the Persian invasions.

While the Greeks of the classical era understood themselves to belong to a common Hellenic [genos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genos),[^cite_note-71] their first loyalty was to their city and they saw nothing incongruous about warring, often brutally, with other Greek [city-states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polis).[^cite_note-72] The [Peloponnesian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peloponnesian_War), the large scale civil war between the two most powerful Greek city-states [Athens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Athens) and [Sparta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sparta) and [their](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delian_League) [allies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peloponnesian_League), left both greatly weakened.[^cite_note-73] A brief [Spartan hegemony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spartan_hegemony), and then a short-lived [Theban hegemony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theban_hegemony), followed up until the [Battle of Mantinea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Mantinea_\(362_BC\)) in 362 BC.



[Alexander the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_the_Great), whose conquests led to the [Hellenistic Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenistic_Age)

After the [rise of Macedon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rise_of_Macedon) and the [Battle of Chaeronea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Chaeronea_\(338_BC\)), most of the feuding Greek city-states became members of the [Hellenic league](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/League_of_Corinth) under the leadership of [Philip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_II_of_Macedon), the [Argead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Argead_dynasty) king of [Macedon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macedon), in order to invade the Achaemenid Empire. The Pan-Hellenic campaign had the slogans of "freeing the Greeks" in Asia and "punishing the Persians" for their past sacrileges during their own invasion of Greece a century and a half earlier. The campaign was led successfully by his son [Alexander the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_the_Great), as Philip was assassinated in 336 BC.[^cite_note-74]

Alexander's toppling of the Achaemenid Empire, after his [victories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wars_of_Alexander_the_Great) at the battles of the [Granicus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_the_Granicus), [Issus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Issus) and [Gaugamela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Gaugamela), and his advance as far as modern-day [Pakistan](23235.md) and [Tajikistan](30108.md),[^cite_note-75] provided an important outlet for Greek culture, via the creation of colonies and trade routes along the way.[^cite_note-76] While the Alexandrian empire did not survive its creator's death intact, the cultural implications of the spread of Hellenism across much of the [Middle East](19323.md) and [Asia](689.md) were to prove long lived as Greek became the *[lingua franca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lingua_franca)*, a position it retained even in [Roman times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_era).[^cite_note-77] Many Greeks settled in [Hellenistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenistic_Greece) cities like [Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandria), [Antioch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antioch) and [Seleucia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seleucia).[^cite_note-78]

#### Hellenistic period

Main articles: [Hellenistic period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenistic_period) and [Hellenistic Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenistic_Greece)



The Hellenistic realms c. 300 BC as divided by the [Diadochi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diadochi); the Μacedonian Kingdom of [Cassander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cassander) (green), the [Ptolemaic Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ptolemaic_Kingdom) (dark blue), the [Seleucid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seleucid_Empire) (yellow), the areas controlled by [Lysimachus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lysimachus) (orange) and [Epirus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epirus) (red)



Bust of [Cleopatra VII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cleopatra_VII) ([Altes Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altes_Museum), [Berlin](3354.md)), the last ruler of a Hellenistic kingdom (apart from the [Indo-Greek Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Greek_Kingdom))

The [Hellenistic civilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenistic_civilization) was the next period of Greek civilization, the beginnings of which are usually placed at Alexander's death.[^cite_note-Boardman364-79] This [Hellenistic age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenistic_period), so called because it saw the partial [Hellenization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenization) of many non-Greek cultures, extending all the way into India and [Bactria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bactria), both of which maintained Greek cultures and governments for centuries.[^cite_note-80] The end is often placed around conquest of [Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ptolemaic_Egypt) by Rome in 30 BC,[^cite_note-Boardman364-79] although the Indo-Greek kingdoms lasted for a few more decades.

This age saw the Greeks move towards larger cities and a reduction in the importance of the city-state. These larger cities were parts of the still larger [Kingdoms of the Diadochi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diadochi).[^cite_note-81][^cite_note-BritHel-82] Greeks, however, remained aware of their past, chiefly through the study of the works of Homer and the classical authors.[^cite_note-Harris-83] An important factor in maintaining Greek identity was contact with *[barbarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barbarian)* (non-Greek) peoples, which was deepened in the new cosmopolitan environment of the multi-ethnic Hellenistic kingdoms.[^cite_note-Harris-83] This led to a strong desire among Greeks to organize the transmission of the Hellenic *[paideia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paideia)* to the next generation.[^cite_note-Harris-83] Greek science, technology and mathematics are generally considered to have reached their peak during the Hellenistic period.[^cite_note-84]

In the [Indo-Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Greeks) and [Greco-Bactrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greco-Bactrian_Kingdom) kingdoms, [Greco-Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greco-Buddhism) was spreading and Greek missionaries would play an important role in propagating it to [China](5405.md).[^cite_note-85] Further east, the Greeks of [Alexandria Eschate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandria_Eschate) became known to the [Chinese people](372983.md) as the [Dayuan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dayuan).[^cite_note-86]

### Roman Empire

Further information: [Roman Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Greece) and [Greco-Roman world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greco-Roman_world)

Between 280 BC and 30 BC, after the [Pyrrhic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pyrrhic_War), [Macedonian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macedonian_War), and [Mithridatic Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mithridatic_Wars), most of the Hellenistic world was conquered by Rome, and almost all of the world's Greek speakers lived as citizens or subjects of the Roman Empire. Despite their military superiority, the Romans admired and became [heavily influenced](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greco-Roman_world) by the achievements of Greek culture, hence [Horace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horace)'s famous statement: *Graecia capta ferum victorem cepit* ("Greece, although captured, took its wild conqueror captive").[^cite_note-87] In the centuries following the Roman conquest of the [Greek world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greece), the Greek and Roman cultures merged into a single [Greco-Roman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greco-Roman) culture.

In the religious sphere, this was a period of profound change. The spiritual revolution that took place, saw a waning of the old Greek religion, whose decline beginning in the 3rd century BC continued with the introduction of new religious movements from the East.[^cite_note-Roberts1-45] The cults of deities like [Isis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isis) and [Mithra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mithra) were introduced into the Greek world.[^cite_note-BritHel-82][^cite_note-BritHelRel-88] Greek-speaking communities of the Hellenized East were instrumental in the spread of early Christianity in the 2nd and 3rd centuries,[^cite_note-89] and Christianity's early leaders and writers (notably [Saint Paul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Paul)) were generally Greek-speaking,[^cite_note-90] though none were from Greece proper. However, Greece itself had a tendency to cling to paganism and was not one of the influential centers of early Christianity: in fact, some ancient Greek religious practices remained in vogue until the end of the 4th century,[^cite_note-91] with some areas such as the southeastern Peloponnese remaining pagan until well into the mid-Byzantine 10th century AD.[^cite_note-92] The region of [Tsakonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsakonia) remained pagan until the ninth century and as such its inhabitants were referred to as *Hellenes*, in the sense of being pagan, by their Christianized Greek brethren in mainstream Byzantine society.[^cite_note-93]

While ethnic distinctions still existed in the [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire), they became secondary to religious considerations, and the renewed empire used Christianity as a tool to support its cohesion and promote a robust Roman national identity.[^cite_note-94] From the early centuries of the [Common Era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_Era), the Greeks self-identified as Romans ([Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval_Greek): Ῥωμαῖοι *Rhōmaîoi*).[^cite_note-95] By that time, the name *Hellenes* denoted pagans but was revived as an ethnonym in the 11th century.[^cite_note-Cameron-96]

### Byzantine Greeks

See also: [Byzantine Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire), [Byzantine Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Greece), [Byzantines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantines), [Fourth Crusade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourth_Crusade), and [Frankokratia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frankokratia)



Scenes of marriage and family life in [Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantinople)



Emperor [Basil II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basil_II) (11th century) is credited with reviving the [Byzantine Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire).



[Gemistos Plethon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gemistos_Plethon), one of the most renowned philosophers of the late Byzantine era, a chief pioneer of the revival of Greek scholarship in Western Europe

During most of the Middle Ages, the Byzantine Greeks self-identified as *Rhōmaîoi* (Ῥωμαῖοι, "Romans", meaning [citizens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_citizenship) of the [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire)), a term which in the [Greek language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language) had become synonymous with Christian Greeks.[^cite_note-Harrison268-97][^cite_note-98] The Latinizing term *Graikoí* (Γραικοί, "Greeks") was also used,[^cite_note-99] though its use was less common, and nonexistent in official Byzantine political correspondence, prior to the Fourth Crusade of 1204.[^cite_note-100] The [Eastern Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire) (today conventionally named the *Byzantine Empire*, a name not used during its own time[^cite_note-BritByz-101]) became increasingly influenced by Greek culture after the 7th century when Emperor [Heraclius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heraclius) (r.  610–641 AD) decided to make Greek the empire's official language.[^cite_note-Haldon50-102][^cite_note-103] Although the [Catholic Church](606848.md) recognized the Eastern Empire's claim to the Roman legacy for several centuries, after [Pope Leo III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_Leo_III) crowned [Charlemagne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charlemagne), king of the [Franks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franks), as the "[Roman Emperor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Roman_Emperor)" on 25 December 800, an act which eventually led to the formation of the [Holy Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Roman_Empire), the Latin West started to favour the Franks and began to refer to the Eastern Roman Empire largely as the *Empire of the Greeks* (*Imperium Graecorum*).[^cite_note-104][^cite_note-105] While this Latin term for the ancient *[Hellenes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greeks)* could be used neutrally, its use by Westerners from the 9th century onwards in order to challenge Byzantine claims to [ancient Roman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Rome) heritage rendered it a derogatory [exonym](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exonym) for the Byzantines who barely used it, mostly in contexts relating to the West, such as texts relating to the [Council of Florence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Florence), to present the Western viewpoint.[^cite_note-106][^cite_note-107] Additionally, among the Germanic and the Slavic peoples, the *Rhōmaîoi* were just called Greeks.[^cite_note-108][^cite_note-109]

There are three schools of thought regarding this Byzantine Roman identity in contemporary [Byzantine scholarship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_studies): The first considers "Romanity" the mode of self-identification of the subjects of a multi-ethnic empire at least up to the 12th century, where the average subject identified as Roman; a perennialist approach, which views Romanity as the medieval expression of a continuously existing Greek nation; while a third view considers the eastern Roman identity as a pre-modern national identity.[^cite_note-110] The Byzantine Greeks' essential values were drawn from both Christianity and the Homeric tradition of [ancient Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greece).[^cite_note-111][^cite_note-Burstein-112]

A distinct Greek identity re-emerged in the 11th century in educated circles and became more forceful after the fall of Constantinople to the Crusaders of the [Fourth Crusade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourth_Crusade) in 1204.[^cite_note-BritIdent-113] In the [Empire of Nicaea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_of_Nicaea), a small circle of the elite used the term "Hellene" as a term of self-identification.[^cite_note-114] For example, in a letter to [Pope Gregory IX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_Gregory_IX), the Nicaean emperor [John III Doukas Vatatzes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_III_Doukas_Vatatzes) (r. 1221–1254) claimed to have received the gift of royalty from Constantine the Great, and put emphasis on his "Hellenic" descent, exalting the wisdom of the Greek people.[^cite_note-115] After the Byzantines recaptured Constantinople, however, in 1261, *Rhomaioi* became again dominant as a term for self-description and there are few traces of *Hellene* (Έλληνας), such as in the writings of [George Gemistos Plethon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Gemistos_Plethon),[^cite_note-116] who abandoned Christianity and in whose writings culminated the secular tendency in the interest in the classical past.[^cite_note-BritIdent-113] However, it was the combination of [Orthodox Christianity](10186.md) with a specifically Greek identity that shaped the Greeks' notion of themselves in the empire's twilight years.[^cite_note-BritIdent-113] In the twilight years of the Byzantine Empire, prominent Byzantine personalities proposed referring to the Byzantine Emperor as the "Emperor of the Hellenes".[^cite_note-Vasiliev-117][^cite_note-CareyCarey1968-118] These largely rhetorical expressions of Hellenic identity were confined within intellectual circles, but were continued by [Byzantine intellectuals who participated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_scholars_in_the_Renaissance) in the [Italian Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Renaissance).[^cite_note-119]

The interest in the Classical Greek heritage was complemented by a renewed emphasis on [Greek Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Orthodox) identity, which was reinforced in the late Medieval and Ottoman Greeks' links with their fellow Orthodox Christians in the [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire). These were further strengthened following the fall of the [Empire of Trebizond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_of_Trebizond) in 1461, after which and until the second [Russo-Turkish War of 1828–29](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Turkish_War_\(1828%E2%80%9329\)) hundreds of thousands of [Pontic Greeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontic_Greeks) fled or migrated from the [Pontic Alps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontic_Alps) and [Armenian Highlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_Highlands) to southern Russia and the Russian [South Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Caucasus) (see also [Greeks in Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greeks_in_Russia), [Greeks in Armenia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greeks_in_Armenia), [Greeks in Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greeks_in_Georgia), and [Caucasian Greeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasian_Greeks)).[^cite_note-120]

These [Byzantine Greeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Greeks) were largely responsible for the preservation of the literature of the classical era.[^cite_note-Burstein-112][^cite_note-Norwich-121][^cite_note-122] [Byzantine grammarians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_scholars_in_the_Renaissance) were those principally responsible for carrying, in person and in writing, ancient Greek grammatical and literary studies to the West during the 15th century, giving the [Italian Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Renaissance) a major boost.[^cite_note-BritRen-123][^cite_note-124] The [Aristotelian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotle) philosophical tradition was nearly unbroken in the Greek world for almost two thousand years, until the [Fall of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Constantinople) in 1453.[^cite_note-125]

To the [Slavic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavic_people) world, the Byzantine Greeks contributed by the dissemination of literacy and Christianity. The most notable example of the latter was the work of the two Byzantine Greek brothers, the monks [Saints Cyril and Methodius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saints_Cyril_and_Methodius) from the port city of [Thessalonica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessalonica), capital of the [theme of Thessalonica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theme_of_Thessalonica), who are credited today with formalizing the [first Slavic alphabet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glagolitic_alphabet).[^cite_note-126]

### Ottoman Greeks

Main articles: [Rum millet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rum_millet), [Romioi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romioi), and [Phanariotes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phanariotes)



The Byzantine scholar and cardinal [Basilios Bessarion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basilios_Bessarion) (1395/1403–1472) played a key role in transmitting classical knowledge to Western Europe, contributing to the Renaissance.



Distribution of ethnic Greek population in Anatolia at the end of the 19th century

Following the [Fall of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Constantinople) on 29 May 1453, many Greeks sought better employment and education opportunities by leaving for the [West](21208200.md), particularly [Italy](14532.md), [Central Europe](5188.md), [Germany](11867.md) and [Russia](25391.md).[^cite_note-BritRen-123] Greeks are greatly credited for the European cultural revolution, later called the Renaissance. In Greek-inhabited territory itself, Greeks came to play a leading role in the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md), due in part to the fact that the central hub of the empire, politically, culturally, and socially, was based on [Western Thrace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Thrace) and [Macedonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macedonia_\(Greece\)), both in [Northern Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Greece), and of course was centred on the mainly Greek-populated, former Byzantine capital, [Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantinople). As a direct consequence of this situation, Greek-speakers came to play a hugely important role in the Ottoman trading and diplomatic establishment, as well as in the church. Added to this, in the first half of the Ottoman period men of Greek origin made up a significant proportion of the Ottoman army, navy, and state bureaucracy, having been levied as adolescents (along with especially [Albanians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albanians) and [Serbs](59512.md)) into Ottoman service through the [devshirme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devshirme). Many Ottomans of Greek (or Albanian or Serb) origin were therefore to be found within the Ottoman forces which governed the provinces, from Ottoman Egypt, to Ottomans occupied [Yemen](350939.md) and [Algeria](358.md), frequently as provincial governors.

For those that remained under the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md)'s [millet system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Millet_\(Ottoman_Empire\)), religion was the defining characteristic of national groups (*milletler*), so the [exonym](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exonym) "Greeks" (*Rumlar* from the name Rhomaioi) was applied by the Ottomans to all members of the [Rum millet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rum_millet) and [Orthodox Church](10186.md), regardless of their language or ethnic origin.[^cite_note-Mazower-127] The [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language) speakers were the only ethnic group to actually call themselves *Romioi*,[^cite_note-128] (as opposed to being so named by others) and, at least those educated, considered their ethnicity (*genos*) to be Hellenic.[^cite_note-129] There were, however, many Greeks who escaped the second-class status of Christians inherent in the Ottoman [millet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Millet) system, according to which Muslims were explicitly awarded senior status and preferential treatment. These Greeks either emigrated, particularly to their fellow Orthodox Christian protector, the [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire), or simply converted to Islam, often only very superficially and whilst remaining [crypto-Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crypto-Christian). The most notable examples of large-scale conversion to Turkish Islam among those today defined as [Greek Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Muslims)—excluding those who had to convert as a matter of course on being recruited through the [devshirme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devshirme)—were to be found in [Crete](6591.md) ([Cretan Turks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cretan_Turks)), [Greek Macedonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Macedonia) (for example among the [Vallahades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vallahades) of western [Macedonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macedonia_\(Greece\))), and among [Pontic Greeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontic_Greeks) in the [Pontic Alps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontic_Alps) and [Armenian Highlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_Highlands). Several Ottoman sultans and princes were also of part Greek origin, with mothers who were either Greek concubines or princesses from Byzantine noble families, one famous example being sultan [Selim the Grim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selim_I) (r.  1517–1520), whose mother [Gülbahar Hatun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G%C3%BClbahar_Hatun_\(mother_of_Selim_I\)) was a [Pontic Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontic_Greek).[^cite_note-130][^cite_note-131]



[Adamantios Korais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adamantios_Korais), leading figure of the [Modern Greek Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Greek_Enlightenment)

The roots of Greek success in the Ottoman Empire can be traced to the Greek tradition of education and commerce exemplified in the [Phanariotes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phanariotes).[^cite_note-BritB-132] It was the wealth of the extensive merchant class that provided the material basis for the intellectual revival that was the prominent feature of Greek life in the half century and more leading to the outbreak of the [Greek War of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_War_of_Independence) in 1821.[^cite_note-BritMerchant-133] Not coincidentally, on the eve of 1821, the three most important centres of Greek learning were situated in [Chios](17067.md), [Smyrna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smyrna) and [Aivali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayval%C4%B1k), all three major centres of Greek commerce.[^cite_note-BritMerchant-133] Greek success was also favoured by Greek domination in the leadership of the [Eastern Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox) church.

### Modern Greece

See also: [Modern Greek Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Greek_Enlightenment), [Greek War of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_War_of_Independence), [Macedonian Struggle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macedonian_Struggle), [Balkan Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balkan_Wars), [Greek refugees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_refugees), [Greco-Italian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greco-Italian_War), [Greek Resistance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Resistance), and [Metapolitefsi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metapolitefsi)

The movement of the Greek enlightenment, the Greek expression of the [Age of Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Enlightenment), contributed not only in the promotion of education, culture and printing among the Greeks, but also in the case of independence from the [Ottomans](22278.md), and the restoration of the term "Hellene". [Adamantios Korais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adamantios_Korais), probably the most important intellectual of the movement, advocated the use of the term "Hellene" (Έλληνας) or "Graikos" (Γραικός) in the place of *Romiós*, that was seen negatively by him.

The relationship between ethnic Greek identity and [Greek Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Church) religion continued after the creation of the modern Greek nation-state in 1830. According to the second article of the first [Greek constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Greece) of 1822, a Greek was defined as any native Christian resident of the [Kingdom of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Greece_\(Wittelsbach\)), a clause removed by 1840.[^cite_note-134] A century later, when the [Treaty of Lausanne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Lausanne) was signed between Greece and Turkey in 1923, the two countries agreed to use religion as the determinant for ethnic identity for the purposes of population exchange, although most of the Greeks displaced (over a million of the total 1.5 million) had already been driven out by the time the agreement was signed.[^cite_note-135][^cite_note-136] The [Greek genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_genocide), in particular the harsh removal of Pontian Greeks from the southern shore area of the Black Sea, contemporaneous with and following the failed Greek [Asia Minor Campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greco-Turkish_War_\(1919%E2%80%931922\)), was part of this process of [Turkification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkification) of the Ottoman Empire and the placement of its economy and trade, then largely in Greek hands under ethnic Turkish control.[^cite_note-137]

In October 1940, [Fascist Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Italy) demanded the surrender of Greece, but it [refused](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ohi_Day), and, in the [Greco-Italian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greco-Italian_War), Greece repelled Italian forces into Albania.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFafaliosHadjipateras1995157-138] French general [Charles de Gaulle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_de_Gaulle) praised the fierceness of the Greek resistance, but the country fell to urgently dispatched [German](21212.md) forces during the [Battle of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Greece). The Nazis proceeded to administer Athens and Thessaloniki, while other regions were given to Fascist Italy and Bulgaria. Over 100,000 civilians died of starvation during the winter of 1941–1942, tens of thousands more died because of reprisals by Nazis and [collaborators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collaboration_with_Nazi_Germany_and_Fascist_Italy#Greece), the economy was ruined, and most [Greek Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Greece) (tens of thousands) were deported and murdered in Nazi concentration camps.[^cite_note-139][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMazower2001155-140] The [Greek Resistance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Resistance), one of the most effective resistance movements, fought against the Nazis. The German occupiers committed [numerous crimes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_war_crimes#Greece), such as atrocities, mass executions, and wholesale slaughter of civilians and destruction of towns and villages, in reprisals. Hundreds of villages were systematically torched and almost one million Greeks left homeless.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMazower2001155-140] The Germans executed around 21,000 Greeks, the Bulgarians 40,000, and the Italians 9,000.[^cite_note-141]

## Identity

### Names

Main articles: [Achaeans (Homer)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achaeans_\(Homer\)) and [Names of the Greeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Names_of_the_Greeks)



Map showing the major regions of mainland ancient Greece, and adjacent "barbarian" lands

The English names *Greece* and *Greek* are derived, via the Latin *_Graecia_* and *_Graecus_*, from the name of the [Graeci](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graecians) (Γραικοί, *Graikoí*; singular Γραικός, *Graikós*), who were among the first [ancient Greek tribes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_ancient_Greek_tribes) to settle [southern Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Italy) (the so-called "[Magna Graecia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magna_Graecia)"). The term is possibly derived from the [Proto-Indo-European](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-Indo-European_language) root *[\*ǵerh₂-](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/Reconstruction\:Proto-Indo-European/%C7%B5erh%E2%82%82-)*, "to grow old",[^cite_note-142][^cite_note-143] more specifically from [Graea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graea) (ancient city), said by [Aristotle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotle) to be the oldest in Greece, and the source of colonists for the [Naples](55880.md) area.[^cite_note-144]



The cover of *[Hermes o Logios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hermes_o_Logios)*, a Greek literary publication of the late 18th and early 19th centuries in [Vienna](55866.md) with major contribution to the [Modern Greek Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Greek_Enlightenment)

Greeks and Greek-speakers have used different names to refer to themselves collectively. The term *Achaeans* (Ἀχαιοί) is one of the [collective names](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Names_of_the_Greeks) for the Greeks in [Homer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homer)'s *[Iliad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iliad)* and *[Odyssey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Odyssey)* (the Homeric "long-haired Achaeans" would have been a part of the [Mycenaean civilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mycenaean_civilization) that dominated Greece from c. 1600 BC until 1100 BC). The other common names are *Danaans* (Δαναοί) and *Argives* (Ἀργεῖοι) while *Panhellenes* (Πανέλληνες) and *Hellenes* (Ἕλληνες) both [appear only once](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hapax_legomenon) in the *Iliad*;[^cite_note-145] all of these terms were used, synonymously, to denote a common Greek identity.[^cite_note-146][^cite_note-147] In the historical period, Herodotus identified the [Achaeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achaea_\(ancient_region\)) of the northern [Peloponnese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peloponnese) as descendants of the earlier, Homeric Achaeans.[^cite_note-148]

[Homer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homer) refers to the "Hellenes" as a relatively small tribe settled in Thessalic [Phthia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phthia), with its warriors under the command of [Achilleus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achilleus).[^cite_note-149] The [Parian Chronicle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parian_Chronicle) says that Phthia was the homeland of the Hellenes and that this name was given to those previously called Greeks (Γραικοί).[^cite_note-Parian-Chronicle-150] In [Greek mythology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_mythology), [Hellen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellen), the patriarch of the Hellenes who ruled around Phthia, was the son of [Pyrrha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pyrrha) and [Deucalion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deucalion), the only survivors after the [Great Deluge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flood_myth).[^cite_note-151] The Greek philosopher [Aristotle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotle) names ancient [Hellas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greece) as an area in [Epirus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epirus) between [Dodona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dodona) and the [Achelous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achelous) river, the location of the Great Deluge of Deucalion, a land occupied by the [Selloi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selloi) and the "Greeks" who later came to be known as "Hellenes".[^cite_note-Aristotle-152] In the Homeric tradition, the Selloi were the priests of Dodonian Zeus.[^cite_note-153]

In the [Hesiodic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hesiod) *[Catalogue of Women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalogue_of_Women)*, [Graecus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graecus) is presented as the son of Zeus and [Pandora II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pandora_II), sister of [Hellen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellen) the patriarch of the Hellenes.[^cite_note-154] According to the [Parian Chronicle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parian_Chronicle), when [Deucalion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deucalion) became king of Phthia, the *Graikoi* (Γραικοί) were named Hellenes.[^cite_note-Parian-Chronicle-150] [Aristotle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotle) notes in his *Meteorologica* that the Hellenes were related to the Graikoi.[^cite_note-Aristotle-152]

### Concepts

The terms used to define Greekness have varied throughout history but were never limited or completely identified with membership to a Greek state.[^cite_note-Broome-155] [Herodotus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herodotus) gave a famous account of what defined Greek (Hellenic) ethnic identity in his day, enumerating&#x20;

1. shared [descent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kinship_and_descent) (ὅμαιμον, *hómaimon*, 'of the same blood')

   [^cite_note-156]

2. shared [language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Language) (ὁμόγλωσσον, *homóglōsson*, 'speaking the same tongue')

   [^cite_note-157]

3. shared [sanctuaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanctuaries) and [sacrifices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacrifices) (θεῶν ἱδρύματά τε κοινὰ καὶ θυσίαι, *theôn hidrúmatá te koinà kaì thusíai*, 'common foundations, common sacrifices to gods')

   [^cite_note-158]

   [^cite_note-159]

4. shared [customs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mores) (ἤθεα ὁμότροπα, *ḗthea homótropa*, 'customs of like fashion').

   [^cite_note-160]

   [^cite_note-161]

   [^cite_note-162]

By Western standards, the term *Greeks* has traditionally referred to any native speakers of the [Greek language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language), whether [Mycenaean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mycenaean_Greek_language), [Byzantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval_Greek) or [modern Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Greek).[^cite_note-Mazower-127][^cite_note-163] [Byzantine Greeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Greeks) self-identified as *Romaioi* ("Romans"), *Graikoi* ("Greeks") and *Christianoi* ("Christians") since they were the political heirs of [imperial Rome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire), the descendants of their [classical Greek forebears](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greeks) and followers of the [Apostles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostles_in_the_New_Testament);[^cite_note-164] during the mid-to-late Byzantine period (11th–13th century), a growing number of Byzantine Greek intellectuals deemed themselves *Hellenes* although for most Greek-speakers, "Hellene" still meant pagan.[^cite_note-Cameron-96][^cite_note-165] On the eve of the [Fall of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Constantinople) the [Last Emperor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantine_XI) urged his soldiers to remember that they were the descendants of Greeks and Romans.[^cite_note-166]

Before the establishment of the modern Greek nation-state, the link between ancient and modern Greeks was emphasized by the scholars of Greek Enlightenment especially by Rigas Feraios. In his "Political Constitution", he addresses to the nation as "the people descendant of the Greeks".[^cite_note-167] The [modern Greek state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Modern_Greece) was created in 1829, when the Greeks liberated a part of their historic homelands, [Peloponnese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peloponnese), from the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md).[^cite_note-168] The large [Greek diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_diaspora) and merchant class were instrumental in transmitting the ideas of western [romantic nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romantic_nationalism) and [philhellenism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philhellenism),[^cite_note-BritMerchant-133] which together with the conception of Hellenism, formulated during the last centuries of the [Byzantine Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire), formed the basis of the [Diafotismos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diafotismos) and the current conception of Hellenism.[^cite_note-BritIdent-113][^cite_note-Mazower-127][^cite_note-169]

The Greeks today are a nation in the meaning of an *[ethnos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_group)*, defined by possessing [Greek culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Greece) and having a Greek [mother tongue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_language), not by citizenship, race, and religion or by being subjects of any particular state.[^cite_note-170] In ancient and medieval times and to some extent today the Greek term was *[genos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genos)*, which also indicates a common ancestry.[^cite_note-171][^cite_note-172]

### Continuity



Alexander the Great in [Byzantine Emperor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Emperor)'s clothes, by a manuscript depicting scenes from his life (between 1204 and 1453)

The most obvious link between modern and ancient Greeks is their language, which has a documented tradition from at least the 14th century BC to the present day, albeit with a break during the [Greek Dark Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Dark_Ages) from which written records are absent (11th–8th cent. BC, though the [Cypriot syllabary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cypriot_syllabary) was in use during this period).[^cite_note-Adrados-173] Scholars compare its continuity of tradition to [Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_language) alone.[^cite_note-Adrados-173][^cite_note-Browning-174] Since its inception, Hellenism was primarily a matter of common culture and the national continuity of the Greek world is a lot more certain than its demographic.[^cite_note-Roberts1-45][^cite_note-ADS-175] Yet, Hellenism also embodied an ancestral dimension through aspects of Athenian literature that developed and influenced ideas of descent based on autochthony.[^cite_note-176] During the later years of the Eastern Roman Empire, areas such as [Ionia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ionia) and [Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantinople) experienced a Hellenic revival in language, philosophy, and literature and on classical models of thought and scholarship.[^cite_note-ADS-175] This revival provided a powerful impetus to the sense of cultural affinity with ancient Greece and its classical heritage.[^cite_note-ADS-175] Throughout their history, the Greeks have retained their language and [alphabet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_alphabet), certain values and cultural traditions, customs, a sense of religious and cultural difference and exclusion (the word *[barbarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barbarian)* was used by 12th-century historian [Anna Komnene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anna_Komnene) to describe non-Greek speakers),[^cite_note-177] a sense of Greek identity and common sense of ethnicity despite the undeniable socio-political changes of the past two millennia.[^cite_note-ADS-175] In recent anthropological studies, both ancient and modern Greek osteological samples were analyzed demonstrating a bio-genetic affinity and continuity shared between both groups.[^cite_note-178][^cite_note-179] There is also a direct genetic link between ancient Greeks and modern Greeks.[^cite_note-Gibbons2017-180][^cite_note-Lazaridis2017-181]

## Demographics

### Ancient

See also: [Colonies in antiquity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonies_in_antiquity)



Greek colonization in antiquity

In ancient times, the trading and colonizing activities of the Greek tribes and city states spread the Greek culture, religion and language around the Mediterranean and Black Sea basins, especially in [Southern Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Italy) (the so-called "[Magna Graecia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magna_Graecia)"), Spain, the [south of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Marseille) and the [Black sea coasts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontian_Greeks).[^cite_note-Apoikiai-182] Under Alexander the Great's empire and successor states, Greek and Hellenizing ruling classes were established in the [Middle East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seleucid_Kingdom), [India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Greek_Kingdom) and in [Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ptolemaic_dynasty).[^cite_note-Apoikiai-182] The [Hellenistic period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenistic_period) is characterized by a new wave of Greek colonization that established Greek cities and kingdoms in [Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dayuan) and [Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyrene,_Libya).[^cite_note-183] Under the Roman Empire, easier movement of people spread Greeks across the Empire and in the eastern territories, Greek became the [lingua franca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lingua_franca) rather than [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin).[^cite_note-Haldon50-102] The modern-day [Griko community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Griko_people) of southern Italy, numbering about 60,000,[^cite_note-Grecia-Salentina-16][^cite_note-Bellinello-17] may represent a living remnant of the ancient Greek populations of Italy.

### Modern

Main articles: [Demographics of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Greece) and [Demographics of Cyprus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Cyprus)

Today, Greeks are the majority ethnic group in the [Hellenic Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenic_Republic),[^cite_note-Greece-184] where they constitute 93% of the country's population,[^cite_note-185] and the [Republic of Cyprus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Cyprus) where they make up 78% of the island's population (excluding Turkish settlers in the occupied part of the country).[^cite_note-autogenerated2-186] Greek populations have not traditionally exhibited high rates of growth; a large percentage of Greek population growth since Greece's foundation in 1832 was attributed to annexation of new territories, as well as the influx of 1.5 million Greek refugees after the [1923 population exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_exchange_between_Greece_and_Turkey) between Greece and Turkey.[^cite_note-187] About 80% of the population of Greece is urban, with 28% concentrated in the city of Athens.[^cite_note-EconWorld-188]



Distribution of ethnic groups in 1918, National Geographic

Greeks from Cyprus have a similar history of emigration, usually to the English-speaking world because of the island's colonization by the [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire). Waves of [emigration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emigration) followed the [Turkish invasion of Cyprus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_invasion_of_Cyprus) in 1974, while the population decreased between mid-1974 and 1977 as a result of emigration, war losses, and a temporary decline in fertility.[^cite_note-Britannica-Cyprus-189] After the [ethnic cleansing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_cleansing) of a third of the Greek population of the island in 1974,[^cite_note-190][^cite_note-191] there was also an increase in the number of Greek Cypriots leaving, especially for the Middle East, which contributed to a decrease in population that tapered off in the 1990s.[^cite_note-Britannica-Cyprus-189] Today more than two-thirds of the Greek population in Cyprus is urban.[^cite_note-Britannica-Cyprus-189]

Around 1990, most Western estimates of the number of ethnic Greeks in Albania were around 200,000 but in the 1990s, a majority of them migrated to Greece.[^cite_note-BJp49-192][^cite_note-193] The Greek minority of [Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greeks_in_Turkey), which numbered upwards of 200,000 people after the 1923 exchange, has now dwindled to a few thousand, after the 1955 [Constantinople Pogrom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_Pogrom) and other state sponsored violence and discrimination.[^cite_note-194] This effectively ended, though not entirely, the three-thousand-year-old presence of Hellenism in Asia Minor.[^cite_note-195][^cite_note-196] There are smaller Greek minorities in the rest of the Balkan countries, the [Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greeks_in_Lebanon) and the [Black Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greeks_in_Georgia) states, remnants of the Old [Greek Diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Diaspora) (pre-19th century).[^cite_note-Prevelakis-197]

### Diaspora

Main article: [Greek diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_diaspora)



Greek diaspora (20th century)

The total number of Greeks living outside Greece and Cyprus today is a contentious issue. Where census figures are available, they show around three million Greeks outside Greece and Cyprus. Estimates provided by the [SAE – World Council of Hellenes Abroad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SAE_%E2%80%93_World_Council_of_Hellenes_Abroad) put the figure at around seven million worldwide.[^cite_note-198] According to George Prevelakis of [Sorbonne University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sorbonne_University), the number is closer to just below five million.[^cite_note-Prevelakis-197] Integration, intermarriage, and loss of the Greek language influence the self-identification of the Greek diaspora (*omogenia*). Important centres include [New York City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Americans), [Chicago](6886.md), [Boston](24437894.md), [Los Angeles](18110.md), [Sydney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Australian), [Melbourne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_community_of_Melbourne), [London](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greeks_in_the_United_Kingdom), [Toronto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_community_of_Toronto), [Montreal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Canadian), [Vancouver](32706.md), [Auckland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_New_Zealander), and [São Paulo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Brazilian).[^cite_note-Prevelakis-197] In 2010, the Hellenic Parliament introduced a law that allowed members of the diaspora to vote in Greek elections;[^cite_note-199] this law was repealed in early 2014.[^cite_note-200]



Poet [Constantine P. Cavafy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantine_P._Cavafy), a native of [Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandria), Egypt

During and after the [Greek War of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_War_of_Independence), Greeks of the diaspora were important in establishing the fledgling state, raising funds and awareness abroad.[^cite_note-201] Greek merchant families already had contacts in other countries and during the disturbances many set up home around the Mediterranean (notably Marseilles in [France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greeks_in_France), Livorno in [Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greeks_in_Italy), Alexandria in [Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greeks_in_Egypt)), [Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greeks_in_Russia) ([Odesa](55545.md) and [Saint Petersburg](24320051.md)), and [Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Greeks) (London and Liverpool) from where they traded, typically in textiles and grain.[^cite_note-Diaspora-202] Businesses frequently comprised the extended family, and with them they brought schools teaching Greek and the [Greek Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Church).[^cite_note-Diaspora-202]

As markets changed and they became more established, some families grew their operations to become [shippers](25159995.md), financed through the local Greek community, notably with the aid of the [Ralli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ralli_Brothers) or [Vagliano Brothers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panayis_Athanase_Vagliano).[^cite_note-Kardasis-203] With economic success, the Diaspora expanded further across the [Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greeks_in_Syria), North Africa, India and the USA.[^cite_note-Kardasis-203][^cite_note-Clogg-204]

In the 20th century, many Greeks left their traditional homelands for economic reasons resulting in large migrations from Greece and Cyprus to the [United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_American), [Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greeks_in_the_United_Kingdom), [Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Australian), [Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Canadian), [Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greeks_in_Germany), and [South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greeks_in_South_Africa), especially after the [Second World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_World_War) (1939–1945), the [Greek Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Civil_War) (1946–1949), and the [Turkish Invasion of Cyprus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_Invasion_of_Cyprus) in 1974.[^cite_note-205]

While official figures remain scarce, polls and anecdotal evidence point to renewed Greek emigration as a result of the [Greek financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_financial_crisis).[^cite_note-206] According to data published by the [Federal Statistical Office of Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Statistical_Office_of_Germany) in 2011, 23,800 Greeks emigrated to Germany, a significant increase over the previous year. By comparison, about 9,000 Greeks emigrated to Germany in 2009 and 12,000 in 2010.[^cite_note-207][^cite_note-208]

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Greece)

[Greek culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Greece) has evolved over thousands of years, with its beginning in the Mycenaean civilization, continuing through the classical era, the Hellenistic period, the Roman and Byzantine periods and was profoundly affected by Christianity, which it in turn influenced and shaped.[^cite_note-Christianity-Hellenism-209] [Ottoman Greeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Greeks) had to endure through several centuries of adversity that culminated in [genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_genocide) in the 20th century.[^cite_note-IAGS-210][^cite_note-211] The [Diafotismos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diafotismos) is credited with revitalizing Greek culture and giving birth to the synthesis of ancient and medieval elements that characterize it today.[^cite_note-BritIdent-113][^cite_note-Mazower-127]

### Language

Main articles: [Greek language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language) and [Greek language question](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language_question)



Early Greek alphabet, c. 8th century BC

A Greek speaker

Most Greeks speak the [Greek language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language), an [independent branch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenic_languages) of the [Indo-European languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-European_languages), with its closest relations possibly being [Armenian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_language) (see [Graeco-Armenian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graeco-Armenian)) or the [Indo-Iranian languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Iranian_languages) (see [Graeco-Aryan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graeco-Aryan)).[^cite_note-Adrados-173] It has the longest documented history of any living language and [Greek literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_literature) has a continuous history of over 2,500 years.[^cite_note-BritLit-212] The oldest inscriptions in Greek are in the [Linear B](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linear_B) script, dated as far back as 1450 BC.[^cite_note-213] Following the [Greek Dark Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Dark_Ages), from which written records are absent, the [Greek alphabet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_alphabet) appears in the 9th–8th century BC. The Greek alphabet derived from the [Phoenician alphabet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoenician_alphabet), and in turn became the parent alphabet of the [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin), [Cyrillic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyrillic), and several other alphabets. The earliest Greek literary works are the [Homeric epics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homer), variously dated from the 8th to the 6th century BC. Notable scientific and mathematical works include [Euclid's Elements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euclid's_Elements), Ptolemy's [Almagest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almagest), and others. The [New Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Testament) was originally written in [Koine Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koine_Greek).[^cite_note-214]

Greek demonstrates several linguistic features that are shared with other [Balkan languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_the_Balkans), such as [Albanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albanian_language), [Bulgarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulgarian_language) and [Eastern Romance languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Romance_languages) (see [Balkan sprachbund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balkan_sprachbund)), and has absorbed many foreign words, primarily of Western European and [Turkish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_language) origin.[^cite_note-215] Because of the movements of [Philhellenism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philhellenism) and the [Diafotismos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diafotismos) in the 19th century, which emphasized the modern Greeks' ancient heritage, these foreign influences were excluded from official use via the creation of [Katharevousa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katharevousa), a somewhat artificial form of Greek purged of all foreign influence and words, as the official language of the Greek state. In 1976, however, the [Hellenic Parliament](2449826.md) voted to make the spoken [Dimotiki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dimotiki) the official language, making Katharevousa obsolete.[^cite_note-216]

[Modern Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Greek) has, in addition to Standard Modern Greek or Dimotiki, a wide [variety of dialects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Varieties_of_Modern_Greek) of varying levels of mutual intelligibility, including [Cypriot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cypriot_Greek), [Pontic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontic_language), [Cappadocian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cappadocian_Greek), [Griko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Griko_language) and [Tsakonian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsakonian_language) (the only surviving representative of ancient [Doric Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doric_Greek)).[^cite_note-217] [Yevanic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yevanic_language) is the language of the [Romaniotes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romaniotes), and survives in small communities in Greece, New York and Israel. In addition to Greek, many Greek citizens in Greece and the diaspora are bilingual in other languages such as English, [Arvanitika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arvanitika)/Albanian, [Aromanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aromanian_language), [Megleno-Romanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megleno-Romanian_language), [Macedonian Slavic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavic_dialects_of_Greece), [Russian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_language) and Turkish.[^cite_note-Adrados-173][^cite_note-218]

### Religion

Main articles: [Religion in ancient Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_ancient_Greece), [Greek Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Church), and [Church of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_Greece)

See also: [Christianity in Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Turkey)



[Christ Pantocrator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christ_Pantocrator) [mosaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mosaic) in [Hagia Sophia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hagia_Sophia), [Istanbul](3391396.md)

Most Greeks are [Christians](18337522.md), belonging to the [Greek Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Church).[^cite_note-abs-219] During the first centuries after [Jesus Christ](1095706.md), the [New Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Testament) was originally written in [Koine Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koine_Greek), which remains the [liturgical language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacred_language) of the Greek Orthodox Church, and most of the early Christians and Church Fathers were Greek-speaking.[^cite_note-Christianity-Hellenism-209] There are small groups of ethnic Greeks adhering to other [Christian](5211.md) denominations like [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_Church) and [Greek Byzantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Byzantine_Catholic_Church) [Catholics](606848.md), [Greek Evangelicals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Evangelical_Church), [Pentecostals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Apostolic_Church_of_Pentecost), [Mormons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mormons), and groups adhering to other religions including [Romaniot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romaniotes) and [Sephardic Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sephardic_Jews), [Greek Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Muslims) and [Jehovah's Witnesses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jehovah's_Witnesses). About 2,000 Greeks are members of [Hellenic Polytheistic Reconstructionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenic_Polytheistic_Reconstructionism) congregations.[^cite_note-220][^cite_note-221][^cite_note-222]

Greek-speaking Muslims live mainly outside Greece in the contemporary era. There are both Christian and Muslim Greek-speaking communities in [Lebanon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greeks_in_Lebanon) and [Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greeks_in_Syria), while in the [Pontus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontus_\(region\)) region of [Turkey](11125639.md) there is a large community of indeterminate size who were spared from the [population exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_exchange_between_Greece_and_Turkey) because of their religious affiliation.[^cite_note-223]

### Arts

Further information: [Greek art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_art), [Music of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Greece), [Ancient Greek architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek_architecture), [Ancient Greek theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek_theatre), [Modern Greek theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Greek_theatre), [Cinema of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Greece), [Modern Greek architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Greek_architecture), and [Modern Greek literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Greek_literature)

See also: [Greco-Buddhist art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greco-Buddhist_art)



Renowned Greek soprano [Maria Callas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maria_Callas)

Greek art has a long and varied history. Greeks have contributed to the visual, literary and performing arts.[^cite_note-Osborne-224] In the West, [classical Greek art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_in_ancient_Greece) was influential in shaping the [Roman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_art) and later the modern [Western artistic heritage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_art_history). Following the [Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance) in [Europe](9239.md), the humanist aesthetic and the high technical standards of Greek art inspired generations of European artists.[^cite_note-Osborne-224] Well into the 19th century, the classical tradition derived from Greece played an important role in the art of the Western world.[^cite_note-225] In the East, [Alexander the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_the_Great)'s conquests initiated several centuries of exchange between Greek, [Central Asian](6742.md) and [Indian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_India) cultures, resulting in [Indo-Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Greek_art) and [Greco-Buddhist art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greco-Buddhist_art), whose influence reached as far as [Japan](15573.md).[^cite_note-226]

[Byzantine Greek art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_art), which grew from the Hellenistic [classical art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fayum_portrait) and adapted the pagan motifs in the service of Christianity, provided a stimulus to the art of many nations.[^cite_note-Mango-Art-227] Its influences can be traced from [Venice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venice) in the West to [Kazakhstan](16642.md) in the East.[^cite_note-Mango-Art-227][^cite_note-228] In turn, Greek art was influenced by eastern civilizations (i.e. [Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_of_ancient_Egypt), [Persia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_art), etc.) during various periods of its history.[^cite_note-229]

Notable modern Greek artists include:&#x20;

- the painters [Dominikos Theotokopoulos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominikos_Theotokopoulos) (El Greco), [Nikolaos Gyzis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikolaos_Gyzis), [Konstantinos Volanakis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konstantinos_Volanakis), [Périclès Pantazis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C3%A9ricl%C3%A8s_Pantazis), and [Constantine Andreou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantine_Andreou); sculptors such as [Yannoulis Chalepas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yannoulis_Chalepas);

- composers such as [Mikis Theodorakis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikis_Theodorakis), [Spyridon Samaras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spyridon_Samaras), [Iannis Xenakis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iannis_Xenakis), [Yanni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yanni) and [Vangelis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vangelis);

- singers such as [Maria Callas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maria_Callas), [Giorgos Dalaras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giorgos_Dalaras), [Nana Mouskouri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nana_Mouskouri), [Vicky Leandros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vicky_Leandros) and [Demis Roussos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demis_Roussos);

- poets such as [Dionysios Solomos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dionysios_Solomos), [Angelos Sikelianos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angelos_Sikelianos), [Andreas Embirikos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andreas_Embirikos), [Constantine P. Cavafy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantine_P._Cavafy) and [Nobel laureates](23385442.md) [Giorgos Seferis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giorgos_Seferis) and [Odysseas Elytis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Odysseas_Elytis);

- the novelists [Alexandros Papadiamantis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandros_Papadiamantis), [Emmanuel Rhoides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emmanuel_Rhoides), [Ion Dragoumis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ion_Dragoumis), [Nikos Kazantzakis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikos_Kazantzakis), [Penelope Delta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penelope_Delta), [Stratis Myrivilis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stratis_Myrivilis), [Vassilis Vassilikos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vassilis_Vassilikos) and [Petros Markaris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petros_Markaris),

- playwrights include the [Cretan Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cretan_Renaissance) poets [Georgios Chortatzis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgios_Chortatzis) and [Vincenzos Cornaros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vitsentzos_Kornaros), and also [Gregorios Xenopoulos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gregorios_Xenopoulos) and [Iakovos Kambanellis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iakovos_Kambanellis).



[Eleftherios Venizelos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eleftherios_Venizelos) was the leading political figure of 20th century Greece.
