Notable cinema or theatre actors include [Marika Kotopouli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marika_Kotopouli), [Melina Mercouri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melina_Mercouri), [Ellie Lambeti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ellie_Lambeti), [Academy Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award) winner [Katina Paxinou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katina_Paxinou), [Alexis Minotis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexis_Minotis), [Dimitris Horn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dimitris_Horn), [Thanasis Veggos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thanasis_Veggos), [Manos Katrakis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manos_Katrakis) and [Irene Papas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irene_Papas). [Alekos Sakellarios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alekos_Sakellarios), [Karolos Koun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karolos_Koun), [Vasilis Georgiadis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vasilis_Georgiadis), [Kostas Gavras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Costa-Gavras), [Michael Cacoyannis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Cacoyannis), [Giannis Dalianidis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giannis_Dalianidis), [Nikos Koundouros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikos_Koundouros) and [Theo Angelopoulos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theo_Angelopoulos) are among the most important directors.

Among the most significant modern-era architects are [Stamatios Kleanthis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stamatios_Kleanthis), [Lysandros Kaftanzoglou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lysandros_Kaftanzoglou), [Anastasios Metaxas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anastasios_Metaxas), [Panagis Kalkos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panagis_Kalkos), [Anastasios Orlandos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anastasios_Orlandos), the naturalized Greek [Ernst Ziller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernst_Ziller), [Dimitris Pikionis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dimitris_Pikionis) and urban planners [Stamatis Voulgaris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stamatis_Voulgaris) and [George Candilis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Candilis).

### Science

See also: [Ancient Greek philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek_philosophy), [Greek mathematics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_mathematics), [Ancient Greek medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek_medicine), [Byzantine science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_science), [Greek scholars in the Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_scholars_in_the_Renaissance), and [List of Greek inventions and discoveries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Greek_inventions_and_discoveries)



[Aristarchus of Samos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristarchus_of_Samos) was the first known individual to propose a [heliocentric system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heliocentrism), in the 3rd century BC.

The Greeks of the Classical and Hellenistic eras made seminal contributions to science and philosophy, laying the foundations of several western scientific traditions, such as [astronomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_astronomy), [geography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography), [historiography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historiography), [mathematics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_mathematics), [medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_medicine), [philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_philosophy) and [political science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_science). The scholarly tradition of the Greek academies was maintained during Roman times with several academic institutions in [Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantinople), [Antioch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antioch), [Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandria) and other centers of Greek learning, while Byzantine science was essentially a continuation of classical science.[^cite_note-230] Greeks have a long tradition of valuing and investing in *paideia* (education).[^cite_note-Harris-83] *Paideia* was one of the highest societal values in the Greek and Hellenistic world while the first European institution described as a university was founded in 5th century Constantinople and operated in various incarnations until the [city's fall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Constantinople) to the Ottomans in 1453.[^cite_note-Bump-231] The [University of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Constantinople) was Christian Europe's first secular institution of higher learning since no theological subjects were taught,[^cite_note-232] and considering the original meaning of the word university as a corporation of students, the world's first university as well.[^cite_note-Bump-231]

As of 2007, Greece had the eighth highest percentage of tertiary enrollment in the world (with the percentages for female students being higher than for male) while Greeks of the Diaspora are equally active in the field of education.[^cite_note-EconWorld-188] Hundreds of thousands of Greek students attend western universities every year while the faculty lists of leading Western universities contain a striking number of Greek names.[^cite_note-233] Notable Greek scientists of modern times include: physician [Georgios Papanicolaou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgios_Papanikolaou) (pioneer in [cytopathology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cytopathology), inventor of the [Pap test](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pap_test)); mathematician [Constantin Carathéodory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantin_Carath%C3%A9odory) (acclaimed contributor to real and complex analysis and the calculus of variations); archaeologists [Manolis Andronikos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manolis_Andronikos) (unearthed the tomb of [Philip II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_II_of_Macedon)), [Valerios Stais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valerios_Stais) (recognised the [Antikythera mechanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antikythera_mechanism)), [Spyridon Marinatos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spyridon_Marinatos) (specialised in [Mycenaean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mycenaean_Greece) sites) and [Ioannis Svoronos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ioannis_Svoronos); chemists [Leonidas Zervas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonidas_Zervas) (of [Bergmann-Zervas synthesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bergmann-Zervas_carbobenzoxy_method) and [Z-group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Z-group) discovery fame), [K. C. Nicolaou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K._C._Nicolaou) (first total synthesis of [taxol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paclitaxel)) and [Panayotis Katsoyannis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panayotis_Katsoyannis) (first chemical synthesis of [insulin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Insulin)); computer scientists [Michael Dertouzos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Dertouzos) and [Nicholas Negroponte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicholas_Negroponte) (known for their early work with the [World Wide Web](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Wide_Web)), [John Argyris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Argyris) (co-creator of the [FEM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finite_element_method)), [Joseph Sifakis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Sifakis) (2007 [Turing Award](31093.md)), [Christos Papadimitriou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christos_Papadimitriou) (2002 [Knuth Prize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knuth_Prize)) and [Mihalis Yannakakis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mihalis_Yannakakis) (2005 [Knuth Prize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knuth_Prize)); physicist-mathematician [Demetrios Christodoulou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demetrios_Christodoulou) (renowned for work on [Minkowski spacetime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minkowski_spacetime)) and physicists [Achilles Papapetrou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achilles_Papapetrou) (known for solutions of [general relativity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_relativity)), [Dimitri Nanopoulos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dimitri_Nanopoulos) (extensive work on particle physics and cosmology), and [John Iliopoulos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Iliopoulos) (2007 [Dirac Prize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dirac_Medal_\(ICTP\)) for work on the [charm quark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charm_quark)); astronomer [Eugenios Antoniadis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/E._M._Antoniadi); biologist [Fotis Kafatos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fotis_Kafatos) (contributor to [cDNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CDNA) cloning technology); botanist [Theodoros Orphanides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theodoros_G._Orphanides); economist [Xenophon Zolotas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xenophon_Zolotas) (held various senior posts in international organisations such as the [IMF](15251.md)); Indologist [Dimitrios Galanos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dimitrios_Galanos); linguist [Yiannis Psycharis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ioannis_Psycharis) (promoter of [Demotic Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demotic_Greek)); historians [Constantine Paparrigopoulos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantine_Paparrigopoulos) (founder of modern Greek historiography) and [Helene Glykatzi Ahrweiler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helene_Ahrweiler) (excelled in [Byzantine studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_studies)); and political scientists [Nicos Poulantzas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicos_Poulantzas) (a leading [Structural Marxist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Structural_Marxism)) and [Cornelius Castoriadis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cornelius_Castoriadis) (philosopher of history and ontologist, social critic, economist, psychoanalyst).

Significant engineers and automobile designers include [Nikolas Tombazis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikolas_Tombazis), [Alec Issigonis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alec_Issigonis) and [Andreas Zapatinas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andreas_Zapatinas).

### Symbols

See also: [Flag of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flag_of_Greece)



The national flag of Greece is commonly used as a symbol for Greeks worldwide.



The flag of the [Greek Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Church) is based on the coat of arms of the [Palaiologoi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palaiologoi), the last dynasty of the [Byzantine Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire).

The most widely used symbol is the [flag of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flag_of_Greece), which features nine equal horizontal stripes of blue alternating with white representing the nine syllables of the Greek national motto *[Eleftheria i Thanatos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eleftheria_i_Thanatos)* (Freedom or Death), which was the motto of the [Greek War of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_War_of_Independence).[^cite_note-234] The blue square in the upper hoist-side corner bears a white cross, which represents [Greek Orthodoxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Church). The Greek flag is widely used by the [Greek Cypriots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Cypriots), although [Cyprus](5593.md) has officially adopted a neutral flag to ease ethnic tensions with the [Turkish Cypriot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_Cypriots) minority (see [flag of Cyprus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flag_of_Cyprus)).[^cite_note-235]

The pre-1978 (and first) flag of Greece, which features a [Greek cross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cross) (*crux immissa quadrata*) on a blue background, is widely used as an alternative to the official flag, and they are often flown together. The [national emblem of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_emblem_of_Greece) features a blue [escutcheon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Escutcheon_\(heraldry\)) with a white cross surrounded by two laurel branches. A common design involves the current flag of Greece and the pre-1978 flag of Greece with crossed flagpoles and the national emblem placed in front.[^cite_note-236]

Another highly recognizable and popular Greek symbol is the [double-headed eagle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flag_of_Greece#Double-headed_eagle), the imperial emblem of the last dynasty of the Eastern Roman Empire and a common symbol in [Asia Minor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asia_Minor) and, later, [Eastern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Europe).[^cite_note-237] It is not part of the modern Greek flag or coat-of-arms, although it is officially the insignia of the [Greek Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Army) and the flag of the [Church of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_Greece). It had been incorporated in the Greek coat of arms between 1925 and 1926.[^cite_note-238]

### Politics

See also: [Politics in Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_in_Greece)

[Classical Athens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Athens) is considered the birthplace of [Democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy). The term appeared in the 5th century BC to denote the political systems then existing in [Greek city-states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_city-state), notably Athens, to mean "rule of the people", in contrast to [aristocracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristocracy) (ἀριστοκρατία, *_aristokratía_*), meaning "rule by an excellent elite", and to [oligarchy](22315.md). While theoretically these definitions are in opposition, in practice the distinction has been blurred historically.[^cite_note-239] Led by [Cleisthenes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cleisthenes), Athenians established what is generally held as the first democracy in 508–507 BC,[^cite_note-240] which took gradually the form of a [direct democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Direct_democracy). The democratic form of government declined during the Hellenistic and Roman eras, only to be revived as an interest in Western Europe during the [early modern period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_modern_period).

The European enlightenment and the democratic, liberal and nationalistic ideas of the [French Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Revolution) was a crucial factor to the outbreak of the [Greek War of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_War_of_Independence) and the establishment of the modern Greek state.[^cite_note-Cl48-241][^cite_note-Paroulakis32-242]

Notable modern Greek politicians include [Ioannis Kapodistrias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ioannis_Kapodistrias), founder of the [First Hellenic Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Hellenic_Republic), reformist [Charilaos Trikoupis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charilaos_Trikoupis), [Eleftherios Venizelos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eleftherios_Venizelos), who marked the shape of modern Greece, social democrats [Georgios Papandreou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgios_Papandreou) and [Alexandros Papanastasiou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandros_Papanastasiou), [Konstantinos Karamanlis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konstantinos_Karamanlis), founder of the [Third Hellenic Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Hellenic_Republic), and socialist [Andreas Papandreou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andreas_Papandreou).

### Surnames and personal names

See also: [Greek name](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_name) and [Ancient Greek personal names](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek_personal_names)

Greek surnames began to appear in the 9th and 10th century, at first among ruling families, eventually supplanting the ancient tradition of using the father's name as disambiguator.[^cite_note-Wickham-243][^cite_note-lexicon-244] Nevertheless, Greek surnames are most commonly patronymics,[^cite_note-Wickham-243] such those ending in the suffix *-opoulos* or *-ides*, while others derive from trade professions, physical characteristics, or a location such as a town, village, or monastery.[^cite_note-lexicon-244] Commonly, Greek male surnames end in -s, which is the common ending for Greek masculine [proper nouns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proper_nouns) in the [nominative case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nominative_case). Occasionally (especially in Cyprus), some surnames end in *-ou*, indicating the [genitive case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genitive_case) of a patronymic name.[^cite_note-245] Many surnames end in suffixes that are associated with a particular region, such as *-akis* (Crete), *-eas* or *-akos* ([Mani Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mani_Peninsula)), *-atos* (island of [Cephalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cephalonia)), *-ellis* (island of [Lesbos](2633994.md)) and so forth.[^cite_note-lexicon-244] In addition to a Greek origin, some surnames have Turkish or Latin/Italian origin, especially among Greeks from [Asia Minor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asia_Minor) and the [Ionian Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ionian_Islands), respectively.[^cite_note-246] Female surnames end in a vowel and are usually the genitive form of the corresponding males surname, although this usage is not followed in the diaspora, where the male version of the surname is generally used.

With respect to personal names, the two main influences are Christianity and classical Hellenism; ancient Greek nomenclatures were never forgotten but have become more widely bestowed from the 18th century onwards.[^cite_note-lexicon-244] As in antiquity, children are customarily named after their grandparents, with the first born male child named after the paternal grandfather, the second male child after the maternal grandfather, and similarly for female children.[^cite_note-247] Personal names are often familiarized by a diminutive suffix, such as *-akis* for male names and *-itsa* or *-oula* for female names.[^cite_note-lexicon-244] Greeks generally do not use middle names, instead using the genitive of the father's first name as a middle name. This usage has been passed on to the [Russians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_names) and other [East Slavs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Slavs) ([otchestvo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otchestvo)).

### Sea: exploring and commerce

Main article: [Greek shipping](25159995.md)



[Aristotle Onassis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotle_Onassis), the best-known Greek shipping magnate worldwide

The traditional Greek homelands have been the Greek peninsula and the Aegean Sea, [Southern Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Italy) (the so called "[Magna Graecia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magna_Graecia)"), the [Black Sea](3386.md), the [Ionian coasts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ionia) of [Asia Minor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asia_Minor) and the islands of [Cyprus](5593.md) and [Sicily](27619.md). In Plato's *[Phaidon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phaedo)*, Socrates remarks, "we (Greeks) live around a sea like frogs around a pond" when describing to his friends the Greek cities of the Aegean.[^cite_note-248][^cite_note-Harl-249] This image is attested by the map of the Old Greek Diaspora, which corresponded to the Greek world until the creation of the Greek state in 1832. The [sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sea) and trade were natural outlets for Greeks since the Greek peninsula is mostly rocky and does not offer good prospects for agriculture.[^cite_note-Roberts1-45]

Notable Greek seafarers include people such as [Pytheas of Massalia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pytheas) who sailed to Great Britain, [Euthymenes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euthymenes) who sailed to Africa, [Scylax of Caryanda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scylax_of_Caryanda) who sailed to India, the [navarch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navarch) of Alexander the Great [Nearchus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nearchus), [Megasthenes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megasthenes), explorer of India, later the 6th century merchant and monk [Cosmas Indicopleustes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cosmas_Indicopleustes) (*Cosmas who sailed to India*), and the explorer of the Northwestern Passage Ioannis Fokas also known as [Juan de Fuca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_de_Fuca).[^cite_note-250] In later times, the Byzantine Greeks plied the sea-lanes of the Mediterranean and controlled trade until an embargo imposed by the [Byzantine emperor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_emperor) on trade with the [Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caliphate) opened the door for the later Italian pre-eminence in trade.[^cite_note-251] [Panayotis Potagos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panayotis_Potagos) was another explorer of modern times who was the first to reach Mbomu and [Uele River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uele_River) from the north.

The Greek shipping tradition recovered during the late Ottoman rule (especially after the [Treaty of Küçük Kaynarca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_K%C3%BC%C3%A7%C3%BCk_Kaynarca) and during the [Napoleonic Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleonic_Wars)), when a substantial merchant middle class developed, which played an important part in the Greek War of Independence.[^cite_note-BritIdent-113] Today, Greek shipping continues to prosper to the extent that Greece has one of the largest merchant fleets in the world, while many more ships under Greek ownership fly [flags of convenience](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flags_of_convenience).[^cite_note-EconWorld-188] The most notable shipping [magnate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnate) of the 20th century was [Aristotle Onassis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotle_Onassis), others being [Yiannis Latsis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yiannis_Latsis), [Stavros G. Livanos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stavros_G._Livanos), and [Stavros Niarchos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stavros_Niarchos).[^cite_note-252][^cite_note-253]

## Genetics

Further information: [Mycenaean Greece § Genetic and anthropometric studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mycenaean_Greece#Genetic_and_anthropometric_studies)

See also: [Genetic history of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_history_of_Europe) and [Roopkund § Human skeletons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roopkund#Human_skeletons)



Admixture analysis of [autosomal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autosomal) [SNPs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Single-nucleotide_polymorphism) of the Balkan region in a global context on the resolution level of 7 assumed ancestral populations: African (brown), South/West European (light blue), Asian (yellow), Middle Eastern (orange), South Asian (green), North/East European (dark blue) and Caucasian/Anatolian component (beige).



Factor correspondence analysis comparing different individuals from European ancestry groups

In their [archaeogenetic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archaeogenetic) study, Lazaridis et al. (2017) found that Minoans and Mycenaean Greeks were genetically highly similar, but not identical; modern Greeks resembled the Mycenaeans, but with some additional dilution of the early Neolithic ancestry. The results of the study support the idea of genetic continuity between these civilizations and modern Greeks, but not isolation in the history of populations of the Aegean, before and after the time of its earliest civilizations. Furthermore, proposed migrations by [Egyptian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Egypt) or [Phoenician](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoenicia) colonists was not discernible in their data, thus "rejecting the hypothesis that the cultures of the Aegean were seeded by migrants from the old civilizations of these regions." The [F<sub>ST</sub>](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fixation_index) between the sampled Bronze Age populations and present-day West Eurasians was estimated, finding that Mycenaean Greeks and Minoans were least differentiated from the populations of modern Greece, Cyprus, Albania, and Italy.[^cite_note-Gibbons2017-180][^cite_note-Lazaridis2017-181] In a subsequent study, Lazaridis et al. (2022) concluded that around \~58.4–65.8% of the ancestry of the Mycenaeans came from [Anatolian Neolithic Farmers (ANF)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_European_Farmers), while the remainder mainly came from ancient populations related to the [Caucasus Hunter-Gatherers (CHG)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasus_hunter-gatherer) (\~20.1–22.7%) and the [Pre-Pottery Neolithic (PPN)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-Pottery_Neolithic) culture in the Levant (\~7–14%). The Mycenaeans had also inherited \~3.3–5.5% ancestry from a source related to the [Eastern European Hunter-Gatherers (EHG)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Hunter-Gatherer), introduced via a proximal source related to the inhabitants of the Eurasian steppe who are hypothesized to be the [Proto-Indo-Europeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-Indo-Europeans), and \~0.9–2.3% from the [Iron Gates Hunter-Gatherers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Gates_Mesolithic) in the Balkans. Mycenaean elites were genetically the same as Mycenaean commoners in terms of their steppe ancestry, while some Mycenaeans lacked it altogether.[^cite_note-254][^cite_note-255]

A genetic study by Clemente et al. (2021) found that in the Early Bronze Age, the populations of the Minoan, [Helladic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helladic_civilization), and [Cycladic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cycladic_culture) civilizations in the Aegean, were genetically homogeneous. In contrast, the Aegean population during the Middle Bronze Age was more differentiated; probably due to gene flow from a Yamnaya-related population from the [Pontic–Caspian steppe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontic%E2%80%93Caspian_steppe). This is corroborated by sequenced genomes of Middle Bronze Age individuals from northern Greece, who had a much higher proportion of steppe-related ancestry; the timing of this gene flow was estimated at \~2,300 BCE, and is consistent with the dominant linguistic theories explaining the emergence of the Proto-Greek language. Present-day Greeks share \~90% of their ancestry with them, suggesting continuity between the two time periods. In the case of Mycenaean Greeks however, their steppe-related ancestry was diluted. The ancestry of the Mycenaeans could be explained via a 2-way admixture model of such MBA individuals in northern Greece, and either an EBA Aegean or MBA Minoan population; the difference between the two time periods could be explained by the general decline of the Mycenaean civilization.[^cite_note-256]

Genetic studies using multiple [autosomal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genealogical_DNA_test#Autosomal_DNA_\(atDNA\)_testing), [Y-DNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genealogical_DNA_test#Y-chromosome_\(Y-DNA\)_testing), and [mtDNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genealogical_DNA_test#Mitochondrial_DNA_\(mtDNA\)_testing) markers, show that Greeks share similar backgrounds as the rest of the Europeans and especially Southern Europeans ([Italians](385155.md) and Balkan populations such as [Albanians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albanians), [Slavic Macedonians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavic_Macedonians) and [Romanians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanians)). A study in 2008 showed that Greeks are genetically closest to Italians and Romanians[^cite_note-257] and another 2008 study showed that they are close to Italians, Albanians, Romanians and [southern Balkan Slavs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Slavs) such as Slavic Macedonians and [Bulgarians](151876.md).[^cite_note-Novembre_et_al_2008-258] A 2003 study showed that Greeks cluster with other South European (mainly Italians) and North-European populations and are close to the [Basques](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basques),[^cite_note-259] and F<sub>ST</sub> distances showed that they group with other European and Mediterranean populations,[^cite_note-Cavalli-Sforza_1996-260][^cite_note-261] especially with Italians (−0.0001) and Tuscans (0.0005).[^cite_note-262] A study in 2008 showed that Greek regional samples from the mainland cluster with those from the Balkans, principally Albanians while [Cretan](6591.md) Greeks cluster with the central Mediterranean and Eastern Mediterranean samples.[^cite_note-King_et_al_2008-263] Studies using mitochondrial DNA gene markers (mtDNA) showed that Greeks group with other Mediterranean European populations[^cite_note-264][^cite_note-265][^cite_note-266] and [principal component analysis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principal_component_analysis) (PCA) confirmed the low genetic distance between Greeks and Italians[^cite_note-267] and also revealed a cline of genes with highest frequencies in the Balkans and Southern Italy, spreading to lowest levels in Britain and the Basque country, which [Cavalli-Sforza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luigi_Luca_Cavalli-Sforza) (1993) associates with "the Greek expansion, which reached its peak in historical times around 1000 and 500 BC but which certainly began earlier".[^cite_note-268] Greeks also have a degree of Eastern-European-related ancestry which is observed in all Balkan peoples; it was acquired after 700 CE, coinciding with the arrival of Slavic-speaking peoples in the Balkans, but the proportion of this ancestry varies considerably between different studies and subregions.[^cite_note-269][^cite_note-270] A 2019 study showed that Cretans share high [IBD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Identity_by_descent) with Western ([CEU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Utah#Birth_data)), Central ([German](152735.md) and [Polish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_people)), Northern (CEU, [Scandinavian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scandinavian_people)) and Eastern Europeans ([Ukrainian](145144.md), [Russian](59510.md)), similar to mainland Greeks who share high IBD with Eastern Europeans. This reflects settlement patterns in Crete, driven by Myceneans and Dorians, Goths and Slavs. Peoples like [Andalusians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andalusians), [Near Eastern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Near_Eastern) [Arabs](2185.md) and [Venetians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Venice) left a minimal genetic impact on Cretans. But a PCA analysis shows that Cretans overlap with [Peloponneseans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peloponnese), Sicilians and [Ashkenazi Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashkenazi_Jews).[^cite_note-271] A 2022 study discovered high genetic affinities between present-day southeastern Peloponnesian populations and Apulians, Calabrians and southeastern Sicilians, which are "all characterised by a cluster composition different from those displayed by other Greek groups", due to low influence from inland populations such as Slavic-related people and/or genetic drift in [Tsakones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsakonia) and [Maniots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maniots). Individuals from western Sicily additionally show similarities with peoples from the western part of the Peloponnese.[^cite_note-272] A 2023 study states that early Cretan farmers shared the same ancestry as other Neolithic Aegeans but received 'eastern' gene flow of Anatolian origin at the end of the Neolithic Age. From the 17th to 12th centuries BCE, genetic signatures of Central and East European ancestry gradually increased in Crete, indicative of mainland Greek influence.[^cite_note-273]

## Physical appearance







Greek warriors, details from painted [sarcophagus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarcophagus) found in Italy, 350–325 BC

A study from 2013 for prediction of hair and eye colour from DNA of the Greek people showed that the self-reported phenotype frequencies according to hair and eye colour categories was as follows: 119 individuals – hair colour, 11 [blond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blond), 45 dark blond/light brown, 49 dark brown, 3 brown red/auburn and 11 had black hair; eye colour, 13 with [blue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue), 15 with intermediate (green, heterochromia) and 91 had brown eye colour.[^cite_note-274]

Another study from 2012 included 150 dental school students from the [University of Athens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Athens), and the results of the study showed that light hair colour (blonde/light ash brown) was predominant in 10.7% of the students. 36% had medium hair colour (light brown/medium darkest brown), 32% had darkest brown and 21% black (15.3 off black, 6% midnight black). In conclusion, the hair colour of young Greeks are mostly brown, ranging from light to dark brown with significant minorities having black and blonde hair. The same study also showed that the eye colour of the students was 14.6% blue/green, 28% medium (light brown) and 57.4% dark brown.[^cite_note-275]

A 2017 study found that Bronze Age Aegean populations had mostly dark hair (brown to black) and eyes. The genetic phenotype predictions matched the visual representations made by the Greeks of themselves, suggesting that art of this period reproduced phenotypes naturalistically.[^cite_note-Lazaridis2017-181]

## Timeline

The history of the Greek people is closely associated with the history of Greece, Cyprus, Southern Italy, Constantinople, Asia Minor and the Black Sea. During the Ottoman rule of Greece, a number of Greek enclaves around the Mediterranean were cut off from the core, notably in Southern Italy, the Caucasus, Syria and Egypt. By the early 20th century, over half of the overall [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language)-speaking population was settled in [Asia Minor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asia_Minor) (now Turkey), while later that century a huge wave of migration to the United States, Australia, Canada and elsewhere created the modern Greek diaspora.

| Time Events  **c. 3rd millennium BC**[Proto-Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-Greek_language) tribes from around the Southern Balkans/Aegean are generally thought to have arrived in the Greek mainland.  **16th century BC**Emergence of the [Achaeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achaeans_\(tribe\)) and formation of the [Mycenaean civilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mycenaean_Greece), which produced the earliest textual evidence of the Greek language.  **15th century BC**[Knossos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knossos) ruled by a Mycenaean elite, who formed a hybrid Mycenaean-Minoan culture on Crete.[^cite_note-\:4-276]  **14th century BC**Mycenaean involvement in [Asia Minor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asia_Minor) begins.[^cite_note-277]  **11th century BC**The Mycenaean civilization ends with destructions of palaces and internal displacements. The [Greek Dark Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Dark_Ages) begin. [Dorians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dorians) move into peninsular Greece.  **9th century BC**Major colonization of Asia Minor and [Cyprus](5593.md) by the Greek tribes.  **8th century BC**First major colonies established in [Sicily](27619.md) and Southern Italy. The first Pan-Hellenic festival, the Olympic games, is held in 776 BC. The emergence of Pan-Hellenism marks the [ethnogenesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnogenesis) of the Greek nation.  **6th century BC**Colonies established across the Mediterranean Sea and the Black Sea.  **5th century BC**Defeat of the Persians and emergence of the Delian League in [Ionia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ionia), the [Black Sea](3386.md) and Aegean perimeter culminates in [Athenian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athenian_Empire) and the [Classical Age of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Greece); ends with Athens defeat by Sparta at the close of the [Peloponnesian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peloponnesian_War)  **4th century BC**Rise of [Theban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thebes_\(Greece\)) power and defeat of the Spartans; [Rise of Macedon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rise_of_Macedon); Campaign of [Alexander the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_the_Great); Greek colonies established in newly founded cities of [Ptolemaic Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ptolemaic_Egypt) and Asia.  **2nd century BC**Conquest of Greece by the [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire). Migrations of Greeks to [Rome](25458.md).  **4th century AD**[Eastern Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire). Migrations of Greeks throughout the Empire, mainly towards [Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantinople).  **7th century**[Slavic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavs) conquest of several parts of Greece, Greek migrations to Southern Italy, Roman emperors capture main Slavic bodies and transfer them to [Cappadocia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cappadocia). The [Bosporus](3705.md) is re-populated by [Macedonian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macedonians_\(Greeks\)) and [Cypriot Greeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Cypriots).  **8th century**Roman dissolution of surviving Slavic settlements in Greece and full recovery of the Greek peninsula.  **9th century**Retro-migrations of Greeks from all parts of the Empire (mainly from Southern Italy and Sicily) into parts of Greece that were depopulated by the Slavic Invasions (mainly western Peloponnesus and Thessaly).  **13th century**Roman Empire dissolves, Constantinople taken by the [Fourth Crusade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourth_Crusade); becoming the capital of the [Latin Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_Empire). Liberated after a long struggle by the Empire of Nicaea, but fragments remain separated. Migrations between Asia Minor, Constantinople and mainland Greece take place.  **15th century – 19th century**Conquest of Constantinople by the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md). [Greek diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_diaspora) into Europe begins. Ottoman settlements in Greece. [Phanariot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phanariot) Greeks occupy high posts in Eastern European millets. | Time Events  **1830s**Creation of the [modern Greek state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_modern_Greece). Emigration to the [New World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_World) begins. Large-scale migrations from Constantinople and Asia Minor to Greece take place.  **1913**European Ottoman lands partitioned; unorganized migrations of Greeks, Bulgarians and Turks towards their respective states.  **1914–1923**[Greek genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_genocide); hundreds of thousands of [Ottoman Greeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Greeks) are estimated to have died during this period.[^cite_note-Rummel-278]  **1919**[Treaty of Neuilly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Neuilly); Greece and Bulgaria exchange populations, with some exceptions.  **1922**[The Destruction of Smyrna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Fire_of_Smyrna) (modern-day Izmir) more than 40 thousand Greeks killed; end of significant Greek presence in Asia Minor.  **1923**[Treaty of Lausanne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Lausanne); Greece and Turkey agree to exchange populations with limited exceptions of the Greeks in [Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantinople), [Imbros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imbros), [Tenedos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tenedos) and the Muslim minority of [Western Thrace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Thrace). 1.5 million of Asia Minor and Pontic Greeks settle in Greece, and some 450 thousands of Muslims settle in Turkey.  **1940s**Hundreds of thousands of Greeks die of starvation during the [Great Famine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Famine_\(Greece\)) caused by the [Axis occupation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axis_occupation_of_Greece).  **1947**[Communist Romania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_Republic_of_Romania) begins evictions of the [Greek community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greeks_in_Romania); approx. 75,000 migrate.  **1948**[Greek Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Civil_War): tens of thousands of [communists](356444.md) and their families flee to [Eastern Bloc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Bloc) nations. Thousands settle in [Tashkent](57533.md).  **1950s**Massive emigration of Greeks to [West Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Germany), the [United States](3434750.md), [Australia](4689264.md), [Canada](5042916.md), and other countries.  **1955**[Istanbul pogrom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_pogrom) against the city's Greeks. [Exodus of Greeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expulsion_of_Istanbul_Greeks) accelerates; fewer than 2,000 remain today.  **1958**Large [Greek community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greeks_in_Egypt) in [Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandria) flees [Nasser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamal_Abdel_Nasser)'s [Arab socialist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_socialism) regime in [Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Egypt_under_Gamal_Abdel_Nasser).  **1960s**[Republic of Cyprus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Cyprus) created as a [sovereign state](23604120.md) under Greek, Turkish and British protection. Economic emigration continues.  **1974**[Turkish invasion of Cyprus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_invasion_of_Cyprus). Almost all Greeks living in northern Cyprus flee to the south or the United Kingdom.  **1980s**Many civil war refugees allowed to return to Greece. Retro-migration of Greeks from Germany begins.  **1990s**[Dissolution of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union). Approximately 340,000 ethnic Greeks migrate from [Georgia](48768.md), [Armenia](10918072.md), southern Russia, and Albania to Greece.  **early 2000s**Some statistics show the beginning of a trend of reverse migration of Greeks from the United States and Australia.  **2010s**Over 200,000 people,[^cite_note-279] particularly young [skilled individuals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brain_drain),[^cite_note-280] emigrate to other EU states due to high unemployment (see also [Greek government-debt crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_government-debt_crisis)).[^cite_note-281] |

[^cite_note-15]: &#x20;["Italy: Cultural Relations and Greek Community"](http://www.mfa.gr/en/greece-bilateral-relations/italy/cultural-relations-and-greek-community.html). *Hellenic Republic: Ministry of Foreign Affairs*. 9 July 2013. [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20160514000227/http://www.mfa.gr/en/greece-bilateral-relations/italy/cultural-relations-and-greek-community.html) from the original on 14 May 2016. Retrieved 4 May 2016. The Greek Italian community numbers some 30,000 and is concentrated mainly in central Italy. The age-old presence in Italy of Italians of Greek descent – dating back to Byzantine and Classical times – is attested to by the [Griko dialect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Griko_dialect), which is still spoken in the [Magna Graecia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magna_Graecia) region. This historically Greek-speaking villages are Condofuri, Galliciano, Roccaforte del Greco, Roghudi, Bova and Bova Marina, which are in the Calabria region (the capital of which is Reggio). The Grecanic region, including Reggio, has a population of some 200,000, while speakers of the Griko dialect number fewer that 1,000 persons.

[^cite_note-Grecia-Salentina-16]: &#x20;["Grecia Salentina"](https://web.archive.org/web/20140819193309/http://www.greciasalentina.gov.it/) (in Italian). Unione dei Comuni della Grecìa Salentina. 2016. Archived from [the original](http://www.greciasalentina.gov.it/) on 19 August 2014. Retrieved 4 May 2016. La popolazione complessiva dell'Unione è di 54278 residenti così distribuiti (Dati Istat al 31° dicembre 2005. Comune Popolazione Calimera 7351 Carpignano Salentino 3868 Castrignano dei Greci 4164 Corigliano d'Otranto 5762 Cutrofiano 9250 Martano 9588 Martignano 1784 Melpignano 2234 Soleto 5551 Sternatia 2583 Zollino 2143 Totale 54278).

[^cite_note-Bellinello-17]: &#x20;[Bellinello 1998](#CITEREFBellinello1998), p. 53: "Le attuali colonie Greche calabresi; La Grecìa calabrese si inscrive nel massiccio aspromontano e si concentra nell'ampia e frastagliata valle dell'Amendolea e nelle balze più a oriente, dove sorgono le fiumare dette di S. Pasquale, di Palizzi e Sidèroni e che costituiscono la Bovesia vera e propria. Compresa nei territori di cinque comuni (Bova Superiore, Bova Marina, Roccaforte del Greco, Roghudi, Condofuri), la Grecia si estende per circa 233 km (145 mi)q. La popolazione anagrafica complessiva è di circa 14.000 unità."

Alexander inherited the idea of an invasion of the Persian Empire from his father Philip whose advance-force was already out in Asia in 336 BC. Philips campaign had the slogan of "freeing the Greeks" in Asia and "punishing the Persians" for their past sacrileges during their own invasion (a century and a half earlier) of Greece. No doubt, Philip wanted glory and plunder.
